We started in French. We’re building for the world, and for something that will outlive any individual, its founder included.

Here’s the bet: A small team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

Reporting directly to the founder and CEO, you will help him run and grow the company.

Your scope is as wide as a CEO/COO’s: strategy, operations, finance, legal, people, product marketing and launches.

The difference is how you do it. In a classic company, the right hand coordinates people and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls. With fewer than 10 people, there is no department to hand things to. You build it.

❌The old way: a chief of staff takes notes in meetings, chases people by email and builds the weekly report by hand.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every CEO decision into execution. Meetings, voice notes and calls become briefs, tickets and follow-ups. Every Monday, the CEO gets one brief: what moved, what’s stuck, and what needs his call.

You decide what the CEO must see and what he never needs to see again. His time goes to vision, product and members, not to coordination.

Depending on your zone of genius, these are examples of strategic projects you could own:

Growth: run experiments with agents that generate, ship and measure them

Partnerships & sponsorships: find and close partners aligned with our brand, for our YouTube channel and events

International: build the machine that turns our French content into English, and open new countries

Launches: take a new offer or product from idea to live

People: find and assess the rare people who join a team of fewer than 10

❌The old way: each new initiative needs a team, a roadmap and three months.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: one person with the right agents launches an offer, a channel or a country without disrupting the core. That’s what our Pirate Squad means now.

❌The old way: an ops manager processes invoices, chases contracts, onboards people and answers the same support questions every week.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: finance, legal, HR, admin and customer support each get their agents, their context, their KPIs and their loop. You write the skills, plug them into our tools and write the evals that define "good": a reconciliation that matches to the cent, a contract that respects our playbook, an answer a member is happy with.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. An agent moves up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month, you build the agent that reconciles payments, refunds and failed charges every night. In month one it only reports. By month three it fixes the simple cases alone, and you only see the exceptions.

❌The old way: someone builds dashboards in a BI tool, exports them to a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody fully trusts the numbers.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: agents watch revenue, cash, acquisition, retention, churn and competitors continuously, find where our sources disagree, and explain what changed and why.

Strategy: screen markets and competition, build growth models, explore business models, partnerships and acquisitions. Then challenge, formalize and ensure good execution of the strategy.

At Paradox things move fast, and our CEO is always reading business books and meeting great entrepreneurs. You’ll turn the best strategies and tactics into systems and agents the whole company uses.

❌The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: every decision, result and lesson goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before acting. The company never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: the share of work done without a human, the time from decision to execution, the agents that moved up an autonomy level this month, and revenue per person. You push those numbers up.

You help the team become AI-native too: you spot the work still done by hand, and you build the agent or show a teammate how to build it.

And always: our members trust us with their lives. No fake claims, no fake scarcity, data handled with care. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. People do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. The company’s output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

Top tier Business or Engineering School (HEC, ESSEC, INSEAD, EM Lyon, EDHEC, ESCP, Stanford, …) or other high-level academic training (Les Mines, X, Sciences Po Paris, etc.)

A wide-scope role behind you: founder, chief of staff, founder’s associate, strategy, consulting or operations in a fast-growing startup

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, agent SDKs, MCP, automations, APIs, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Driven with strong work ethic: this position requires a strong commitment and perseverance

Entrepreneurship spirit: able to own a project from scratch, with few resources and little guidance, doing a lot yourself

Builder, not coordinator: you’d rather build the agent than chase a person twice

Taste: you know the difference between an output that works and one that is great, and you hold agents to the second

Growth over ego: you are at ease with regular feedback, obsessed with learning and growing, able to put your ego aside

Analytical and system thinker: you quickly identify and formulate problems, and solve complex ones with data

Excels in communication: both written and verbal

French: native or bilingual. English: fluent, you can hold a detailed conversation with English-speaking colleagues. Our chosen language for written communication is English.

We are inspired by a culture of performance and ambition. Everything is fast-paced and there is a lot of change. You won’t always be told what to do or how to do it.

We tell each other things, even if they are uncomfortable. We value growth over ego. At Paradox, expressing disagreement to a manager and knowing how to debate is valued! We encourage critical and strategic thinking.

❌ If you use AI for first drafts but your way of working looks the same as two years ago: wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage a lot of people: we aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each managing a dozen agents.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos: wrong place.

If all this makes sense with you, then we’ll love working with you (and you with us, you’ll be pampered!)

Build one of the first truly AI-native companies from inside the CEO’s office, and write the playbook others will copy. You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

🏅Being surrounded by A-players who strive to perform, whilst being in a good mood

🏋️‍♀️No more bosses, but ongoing conversations to support your growth

⚡️Learning to build with AI faster than almost anywhere else

🦄Playing a crucial role in the development of a company whose content and products are used by millions of people

🤩Waking up in the morning feeling like you are making a difference is something! We receive messages every day from members who quit smoking, reached a milestone in their business, built better relationships and changed their lives.

But that’s not all, we believe that driven people deserve…

😎A company culture based on autonomy & accountability

#6987. Software Engineer - Interfaces Backend at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer to join Supabase’s Interfaces group and help run the backend services behind our developer tools, starting with postgres-meta: the open-source service that runs SQL against customers’ Postgres databases on behalf of our dashboard, our MCP server, AI agents, and other platform tools. You’ll work closely with the engineers who build those tools, and with our Platform and SRE teams.

AI agents are the fastest-growing source of postgres-meta’s traffic, and it sits on the path between developers and their data, so when it struggles, people can’t reach their databases from the dashboard or their agents. The team’s first priority is making it reliable, observable, and fair between tenants at agent scale, and helping redesign it for that scale without breaking the tools that depend on it. Depending on your experience, you’ll own major parts of that work or lead it. Alongside it, the team builds the backend services that give every branch of a project the configuration and secrets it needs.

We want to be upfront: this role carries real operational load. Our on-call process and rotation are new and need a lot of maturing, and you’ll help build them into something sustainable from your first weeks. You’ll be on call for services on the critical path, and incidents come before planned work. Platform teams run the infrastructure underneath; you’ll own the services themselves, from their code to how they behave in production. It’s ideal for someone who enjoys that kind of ownership, thrives in async, fast-paced environments, and stays calm when the root cause turns out to be in someone else’s system.

Help mature the team’s on-call from your first weeks: grow the rotation to cover every service the team runs, write runbooks, and agree when to declare an incident. Then build it, with our embedded SRE, into a sustainable follow-the-sun rotation as the team grows.

Set SLOs with the teams that call the service, and build the observability to hold them: metrics per tenant and per caller, alerts that catch failures wherever they start, and logs you can search across regions.

Make deploys routine with canary and staged rollouts, so dependency and security upgrades stop being risky.

Redesign how the service works with Postgres at scale: reusing connections, limiting or streaming large results, and isolating and rate-limiting tenants, so no single caller, human or agent, can degrade it for everyone else.

Help decide the service’s long-term architecture with our Platform teams, then evolve it without breaking the callers that depend on it.

Write down and agree the service’s contract with its callers, including the dashboard, MCP and AI, and platform workers: how transactions, multi-statement queries, timeouts, and errors behave.

Work through incidents with the networking, Kubernetes, pooler, and control-plane teams when a problem on the path to customers’ databases starts in their systems.

Build backend services for code-first workflows, starting with delivering each branch’s configuration and secrets to the services that need them.

Make small changes directly in the CLI, dashboard, or docs when a capability needs them, with the owning team reviewing.

Review code and designs, mentor other engineers, and grow into leading the team’s technical direction.

Have 5+ years of experience as a software engineer, most of it building and running backend services in production.

Have run a high-traffic, multi-tenant service in production: been on call for it, worked incidents through to resolution, and followed through on postmortems.

Write strong TypeScript, and work confidently in a large Node.js backend codebase.

Understand Postgres from the client side: connection management and pooling, transactions, statement timeouts and cancellation, and large result sets.

Have built observability for a service you owned (metrics, tracing, logs, and alerts tied to SLOs), and used it to debug problems that span proxies, load balancers, or Kubernetes.

Have kept a service healthy under load with patterns such as rate limiting, timeouts, memory limits, and graceful degradation.

Have shipped changes to a live service safely, with canaries, staged rollouts, feature flags, or fast rollback.

Work calmly and constructively with other teams during incidents, including when the root cause is in a system you don’t own.

Communicate clearly in writing, from design docs to postmortems, thrive in async, globally distributed teams, and make progress when the path isn’t fully defined.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have rebuilt or migrated a live, high-traffic service without breaking its callers, using techniques such as shadow traffic, contract tests, or incremental replacement.

Have led the design of reliability work, such as tenant isolation, load shedding, or a staged-deploy pipeline, or set technical direction that other engineers built on.

Have built or operated a database proxy or connection pooler (such as PgBouncer, Supavisor, or pgcat), or an API gateway such as Envoy.

Have secured a service that runs SQL on its callers’ behalf: handling credentials safely, and limiting how much damage any one caller can do.

Production experience in a second backend language, such as Elixir or Go.

Experience with a secrets manager or key management service, such as HashiCorp Vault or AWS KMS.

Familiarity with Supabase, or with how AI agents use databases through tools like MCP.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#6985. Account Manager at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured American to get life-changing aid. Our mission is to tear down barriers between people in crisis and the aid they deserve. In the last six years, we’ve become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We also help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we’ve helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10 billion in life-changing aid —and earned over 20,000 five-star reviews. And we’re just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, Google Ventures, and True Ventures, and we’re on our way to creating a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

In the next two years, we aim to help over 250,000 Americans get access to life-changing benefits. Much of that help reaches people through our Lawyer Network: our network of vetted partner law firms that represent clients alongside Atticus. The Lawyer Network (LN) team, led by Erin Casale, is the bridge between Atticus and those firms, and our job is to improve their ability to serve clients and help them grow.

We're hiring a Senior Account Manager to own our largest partner firms. You'll carry a deliberately small book of high-volume firms (roughly 10) and act as an extension of their team: diagnosing where and why they lose cases, coaching them on operations and legal execution, and getting even the most technophobic firm to adopt new Atticus tools.

The ideal candidate has spent years making small business customers measurably better at what they do through a mix of operations and tech, and wants to do that work somewhere it changes lives.

Make firms better. Own our enterprise-tier partner firms and improve their capacity, conversion rates, and win rates. Find best practices in firm operations and Social Security Disability legal execution, spread them across the network, and coach them relentlessly.

Drive technology adoption. Get every firm, including folks who are tech-hesitant, using new Atticus features well. That means making the case for the value (think: sales motion but the product is free), building and implementing change management plans, and running training and support.

Know your firms from the inside. Build relationships beyond the managing attorney, down to intake, case development, and operations leads. Understand a firm well enough to help with hiring, software, and staffing decisions, not just Atticus problems.

Be the voice of firms inside Atticus. Bring what you learn back to Product, Customer Experience, and Operations so we serve firms better and learn from their experience.

Deliver hard news well. Hold firms to clear performance standards and push back on attorneys—even those with decades of experience in the field—with finesse and confidence. If needed, cut ties gracefully.

Shape how the team works. Own strategic projects end to end, from scoping the question and gathering data to sharing recommendations with leadership and rolling out what works across the network.

5+ years in Account Management, Customer Success, Partnerships, or a similar role serving small businesses, where your mandate was improving your accounts' performance (not just retention)

A track record of driving product adoption in a tech or tech-adjacent setting

You read data comfortably, spot trends before results move, and show up with both a diagnosis and a plan

You're persuasive in conversation and in writing, and you'd rather pick up the phone (or get on a plane) than send another email

You're outgoing and build trust quickly, with colleagues and with skeptical partners alike

Adaptable: resourceful and steady when priorities shift

You pick up new tools fast, from cloud software to building a clean deck

We covered above why this job might be for you, and think it’s equally important to tell you why it might not be for you. You won’t enjoy this job very much if:

You prefer a large book of light-touch accounts over depth with a few

You get frustrated when projects and priorities change quickly (we obviously try to limit this, but are a quickly-growing company and it does still happen)

The role is a rare opportunity to join a fast-growing Series C startup that doubles as a B-corp social enterprise. Our team is still small enough for you to have an outsized impact on how we operate and how our firms grow.

We offer competitive pay, including equity, and generous benefits:

Medical and dental insurance with 100% of employee and family premiums covered

15 vacation days and ~20 paid holidays each year (including two weeks at end-of-year)

$1,000 reimbursable stipend for education and training outside of work

Up to $1,200/year student loan repayment assistance

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

We anticipate the base salary band for this role will be between $110,000 and $130,000, in addition to equity and benefits. The salary at offer will be determined by a number of factors such as the candidate's experience, knowledge, skills and abilities, as well as internal equity among our team.

This job is fully remote and we're committed to empowering everyone with flexibility. Work remotely, and travel to LA (on the company dime) as needed to be with your colleagues, usually quarterly, plus offsites. We care a lot about building a great culture and we think some interactions need to happen in person, so we put a lot of thought into retreats, offsites, and other ways to gather. You'll also visit partner firms in-person occasionally. Overall, expect to travel up to monthly between internal get togethers and firm visits.

#6982. Inside a Modern Hamptons Home With Views in Nearly Every Direction

Leroy Street Studio designed this Water Mill retreat around a large blended family—and a landscape that changes with every season.

It’s hard to pick a favorite time of year at Peninsula House. Nicknamed for its location on a point overlooking Mecox Bay in Water Mill, New York, the residence was designed by the architecture firm Leroy Street Studio to capitalize on the site’s 270-degree views. An array of indoor and outdoor vantage points capture the ever-changing cinematic interplay of light and water as well as nature’s shifts from season to season.

The owners, a couple with a large blended family, wanted a multigenerational gathering spot that they and their young daughter could share with the husband’s adult children and their spouses as well as friends. From the outset, the clients made it clear they were looking for out-of-the-ordinary architecture that is thoughtfully, creatively integrated into its unique setting. “They really liked the idea of doing something that was countercurrent for the Hamptons,” says Morgan Hare, the Leroy Street founding partner who headed the project.

Adopting a pinwheel-type plan, the striking 14,000-square-foot house features multiple wings radiating from a central core. These spaces vary in scale and height, and some feature deep overhangs, providing shade and amplifying the design’s dynamic horizontality. “The oversailing roofs create a solar brim that helps keep the rooms cool in the summer,” says Alex Witko, a senior associate at Leroy Street who served as the project lead. “They also amplify the views, including from the primary bedroom, where you can just sit and watch the herons on the nearby spit of land that is a protected wildlife preserve.”

Softening the formal rigor, most of the house is topped with lushly planted green roofs and terraces that offer distinct vistas, while the extensively glazed exterior is clad in handmade white brick on the lower levels and dark-stained wood above. The black and white palette is one that Leroy Street, which has designed dozens of waterfront homes in the Hamptons and elsewhere, has rarely used previously. “The dark wood, somewhat counterintuitively, blends into and dematerializes against the vegetation,” Hare says, noting how the house opens on all sides to tree-shaded terraces and courtyards.

Landscape architect Ed Hollander and his firm Hollander Design collaborated on the 1.7-acre property’s outdoor living spaces, while outfitting the gardens with what he calls “maritime habitat” plantings, a mix of native and non-native species “that are adapted to this sunny, salty, breezy environment.”

Everything in the house’s design was conceived around creating a seamless flow—visual and literal—between inside and outside. The main entry consists of a two-story glass-walled gallery that connects the two largest wings, providing views from the front walkway through the house to a rear garden planted with ginkgo trees and the bay beyond. “In the morning, the sun actually comes up through that ginkgo grove and reflects off the entry hall’s glass pendant lights,” notes Hollander. “Then in the evening, the lights become like starlight, these magic things that dance against the trees.”

Inside, the light-and-dark palette continues with the white artisanal brick, expanses of warm plaster, and pale oak and stone offset by inky-hued wood and blackened-steel accents. Throughout, the predominantly new and custom furnishings, overseen by Eleanor Donnelly of Stelle Lomont Rouhani Architects, stick to a sculptural, pared-down aesthetic, complemented by a contemporary art collection that includes works by Teresita Fernández, Sarah Morris, and Adam McEwen. Elevated details like bespoke integrated door pulls and closets with facings in faux-shagreen or mottled resin add subtle character.

The horseshoe-shaped main stairwell is one of few rounded architectural elements in the house. With floating treads and a minimalist oculus at the top, there’s a play of light, shadow, and form that shifts as one moves between floors.

Reaching the home’s dramatic second-story rooftop terrace requires taking a separate, private flight of stairs. A seductive spot for sunsets and stargazing, it features a seating area with a fire bowl surrounded by wispy native grasses and plants.

While a major appeal of this house is all of its intimate spaces for quiet escape, equally important was having versatile areas for gathering in larger groups. That might mean bringing two dozen guests together around the formal dining tables that can be combined and separated as needed or congregating in the various outdoor spaces around the expansive pool area.

Step Inside This Waterfront Hamptons Home

Hare notes that while the firm has designed plenty of residences for extended families, they have typically taken the form of compounds with separate structures. “This is the first one we've done that’s multigenerational and all in one building,” he says. “It was a big part of our mission—and, ultimately, the success of the house.”

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

#6978. The Inconvenient Truth About the Electricity AI Requires

AI companies are facing political backlash, but they’re staring down an even bigger problem as they look to take over the world.

He-Man liked to exclaim: “I have the power!” AI should be so lucky.

When it comes to the wielders of artificial intelligence, and the data centers needed to fuel it, language and numbers become inescapably extreme: trillions in investment, gigawatts of electricity, the extinction of the human race. It’s almost cartoonish, until you get down to some brass tacks: The power is not there to fuel these dreams of the would-be Masters of the Universe.

“The U.S. grid is not designed for large data-center loads,” says Anubhav Arora, senior director of global infrastructure at Fitch and a coauthor of a recent paper the ratings agency published on data centers. “Securing reliable, sufficient power is the single greatest bottleneck in data-center development,” the paper’s authors write. (Esquire and Fitch share the same parent company, Hearst.)

In the U.S.—which is by far the world leader in data centers—electricity demand from the facilities could reach up to 649 terawatt-hours (TWh) a year by 2030, according to a 2026 Berkeley Lab analysis, or roughly 12 percent of all U.S. electricity consumption. Powering New York City for an entire year requires one tenth that energy.

Meeting global demand for compute could require nearly $7 trillion in capital investment by 2030, including $5.2 trillion for AI-related data centers, according to McKinsey estimates. Estimates from Goldman Sachs Research say that Europe will need to invest more than $3.5 trillion on its grid in the next ten years to cope with increased demand. (China looks to be fine, but it is slow to the data-center boom.)

And the problem is not just a lack of juice. “The actual grid infrastructure . . . is also basically limited in terms of transmission capacity, and the interconnection process is a little bit outdated,” says Arora. That grid—the network of transmission lines, transformers, and power plants that create and distribute electricity to hundreds of millions of users—is constrained, and the interconnection backlog is massive. Put another way: Not only is the shop running low on milk, but the line to get inside is stretching around the block. “You could build a data center in one and a half to two years, but to get interconnection, it takes five to ten years,” Arora says.

According to Cleanview, a company that tracks U.S. power infrastructure, there are currently 1,454 operating data centers, as well as up to 578 under construction and 2,663 proposed. Companies like Alphabet, Meta, and Anthropic are scrambling. With that scramble, there is money to be made. PJM, the largest power-grid operator in the United States, has been trying to devise a new way to accommodate the data-center boom. Its proposed “Bring Your Own New Capacity” (or BYONC) framework would have allowed new data-center projects to buy energy in the form of a battery and add it to the grid. But in October, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission rejected that portion of PJM’s proposal, sending the grid operator back to the drawing board.

Important here is what kind of data centers we’re talking about. Of the more than 3,200 data centers proposed globally, 800 of them are hyperscalers. These are the biggies. The campuses. Sprawling centers with square footage in the millions and power demands upwards of 100 MW (which could power 100,000 homes). Collectively, some estimates put the global power usage at 200 TWh annually. (That’s juice for 11 million homes.)

How can companies even think about the grid beginning to handle that demand? Increasingly, they aren’t. Many hyperscalers are looking to “bring your own power [BYOP],” says Jonathon Smith, associate director of Sustainable Finance Research at Fitch. In other words, build their own power plants. The problem here is twofold: First, very few people on the planet know how to build something like this. You could buy the biggest army the world has ever seen; try moving it without engineers. Second, it poses very real and very dangerous environmental threats.

Recently, the journal Nature Sustainability published a study revealing that the annual carbon dioxide emissions of the largest data centers in the U.S. are equal to that of a small country, like Paraguay or Denmark. And that study looked at data centers from 2024 and 2025. Data centers have exploded since then, with as much as a 190 percent increase from 2025 to 2026.

When companies like Anthropic say, “Don’t worry, we’re not even touching the grid,” it brings up a cartoon situation more akin to The Simpsons than a Saturday-morning serial. Picture it: a giant data center, clean, cool, not an emission in sight, just humming machines and maybe a security guard. But there’s a pipe going out the back, and if you follow it as it snakes around the corner, it connects to a behemoth power plant, burning enough natural gas to power a city, with plumes of smoke assaulting the sky above. Animated, but closer to the reality of BYOP.

“From an environmental-impact perspective, most of these developers are deploying natural-gas-based assets for power, which release GHG [greenhouse gas] emissions and air pollutants,” says Smith. The cleaner alternatives are renewables (unlikely), nuclear (terrifying), and batteries (no environmental picnic, either).

Among the environmental concerns—which inevitably bleed into the social and economic ones—are these centers’ use of water, which they need in great volume to keep those humming stacks of servers cool. While many experts point to these issues as overblown, especially as data centers look to closed-loop systems (there is an initial dump of water that gets cooled and reused), the reason you won’t stop hearing about water use is because 1) it’s concerning, and 2) water is inherently political and always has been. (Traditionally, we’ve needed the stuff to feed our animals, grow our crops, and, you know, live.) Go back to the early chapters of the Bible and what’s causing strife? Water. Go back even further and some of the first wars ever recorded—4,500 years ago in ancient Sumeria—are over the stuff. And data centers have become a fast-moving political football.

A recent Gallup poll indicates that 71 percent of Americans do not want an AI data center near where they live. Those are some very big numbers. It’s important to clarify that the data centers big tech wants to build near dense populations are for cloud computing, and they make up roughly 70 percent of all data centers. While centers for AI computing and AI training are catching up, these can be built farther away, for the simple reason that we’re happy to wait a few minutes for AI to do our taxes but not for the cloud to recombobulate an email or Instagram post.

That’s still a huge number of data centers that need to be near you and me, so what will we do about it? The politicians are listening. New York State has issued a moratorium. There’s legislation limiting the centers on the table in nine other states. Gina Hinojosa, who just pummeled Greg Abbott in the Governor's race in Texas, campaigned on promises of a moratorium for permitting and construction on new data centers. Norway, the Netherlands, and Denmark are leading the charge on moratoriums and other restrictions in Europe as governments catch up with popular opinion.

Moreover, back in the U.S.A., Donald Trump has touted a “deal” with big tech on data centers, the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, supposedly protecting grids from the biggies. But never has Tip O’Neill’s maxim that “All politics is local” been more salient. More than 500 localities are forming their own moratoriums, zoning freezes, and permit pauses. Even after the midterms, how and if to move forward with data centers is a debate for states, counties, cities, townships, and any other geographic entity that makes laws, collects taxes, and breathes collective air.

As the Democrats take over Congress and data centers become every pol’s favorite new cudgel, as the European Union and member states enact regulatory frameworks (often tied to renewable energy and climate), as elected officials turn away from AI’s once sunnily vague promises, something is becoming clear: It’s humans who have the power.

#6966. Everything but the Plane: Here’s How One Brand Is Bringing Glamour Back to Travel

PS’s private terminals are the epitome of real luxury.

Imagine a trip to the airport with no lines. No lines to check in, to drop your bag, to be told your first-class ticket is the wrong first-class ticket for the first-class lounge. Now add to that no interpersonal friction. There are no snappy desk workers, no turbo TSA employees, and no graveyard-shift customs agents that give you grief.

Airlines can solve only so many issues. These are terminal problems. PS, a Los Angeles–based company operating its own luxury private terminals, is solving them. The goal is to get you as close to the charter experience as possible, all while still flying commercial. If you ask around or sit in the common areas and eavesdrop long enough, you’ll likely hear something along the lines of “I usually fly private, but when I don’t, this is what I use.”

The company has locations in Atlanta, Dallas Fort Worth, Miami, and Los Angeles, and the next order of business is expansion to the airports its clients use most—business hubs, mostly. But it started at LAX in 2017, and that’s the perfect airport for something like this. The horseshoe design and the traffic it creates, the small terminals, and the paparazzi culture do not cater to the A-list and executive frequent fliers that use it as a home airport. PS offers a way around that with on-tarmac connection from your flight to its terminal and vice versa. A chauffeur pulls you straight off the jet bridge and into a luxury fleet vehicle; that partnership is currently with BMW. If you don’t have any checked luggage, that 7 Series can (for an additional price) drive you straight to your destination within 35 miles of the airport. So if you are sitting in row 1 of first class, that’s about 50 steps between you and the i7 that takes you to your hotel. Or it can take you back to the PS terminal, which is not a lounge but acts as an über-private lounge experience.

It ain’t cheap: $1,300 per head one-way for the Salon (a shared bar area), $4,950 for a private suite (essentially an airport hotel room with butler and spa service; this price is all-inclusive), and $1,650 for that PS Direct drive-you-straight-home service. There are discounts and perks if you opt for yearly membership, but this is all on top of your ticket, folks.

And it can lean comically luxurious, with Champagne and caviar offered every time you enter—like before my 8:00 a.m. departure, to which I’m not opposed but which caught me a little off guard. There’s the jet-set marketing—tagline: The “back door” to the airport—and meant-for-the-timeline branded cocktail napkins: “You’ve arrived.”

But once you accept that PS could not afford to excuse itself from the flex-driven airport exclusivity economy, you can really admire its hospitality-inspired finishes. Its raison d’être is to get you through the airport with as little friction as possible, but it stands out in its commitment to hospitality within those confines.

For example, interior design and artwork are curated. The centerpiece artworks of each location are done in collaboration with local artists, made to fit the location they are in. The crown jewel thus far is in Miami, where PS took over the old Pan Am headquarters and flight-training academy. The mid-century building is a Miami landmark, with geometric thermal walls acting as the facades and this pinkish sandy and limestone-heavy concrete throughout. Argentinian-born ceramicist Nina Surel matched this with tile-work water features and a ceramic mural at the center of the property. Design like this, small and mostly unnoticed in the grand scheme of things, is what turns your mind back to a golden age of travel. It’s not credit card points and moneymaking schemes—though, actually, you can buy any of the non-centerpiece artworks—just beauty for the sake of beauty.

On top of that, the food and beverage program is considered and regional. The blue-corn cornbread with honey and a ricotta spread that chef Kelsey Kirwin (chefs’ names are on the menus, an indication of the atmosphere) is making at the DFW location overtook my mother’s as my all-time favorite. I would consider the signature PS martini—with (guess what) caviar-stuffed olives—worthy of a place on any hot big-city cocktail menu. And all the coffees, snacks, and water options are head and shoulders above those of both the general-population terminals and the litany of airport lounges within them.

As far as service, yes, an agent handles your party’s check-in and keeps you abreast of delays so you don’t have to open an app—but that’s to be expected. It’s more in the interim. Servers don’t hover, they listen, and they’re backed by an extensive behind-the-scenes operation: Mention that you prefer coffee, not bubbles, before a morning flight? A note is made on your profile for the next time you visit. Only ever ask for sparkling water when you fly out of LAX? They’ll know that in Atlanta. The entire operation seems to run on Guidara’s Unreasonable Hospitality, the same way all modern restaurants do. If you’re leaving Miami and mention to a business partner that you forgot to try a Cubano (on menu) and a cafecito (off menu), both will likely be delivered to your table, compliments of the house.

But every time I start to get wowed by the service details, I remember the price point and the level of clientele. That should be expected.

Standard terminals are air-side, post-security, where everything (even a fancy and expensive airline lounge) is working within those confines. Since PS operates land-side, with security the last stage of the terminal experience, it has the flexibility to be more hospitable to guests. For example, if you book a private suite, you can fly in, use it for a business meeting, and fly out. Or your parents can accompany the family to departure, hang out in the suite, and squeeze every last second with the grandkids out of your visit. It is an airport experience actually designed around the ways we interact and what matters to us when we travel. That, more than the Champagne and caviar, is the definition of luxury.

#6965. These Are the Home Trends Gaining Ground for 2027, According to Zillow

A new analysis of millions of for-sale listings reveals the features sellers are increasingly highlighting.

Above: A color-drenched den on Long Island, New York, designed by General Assembly.

Homes are getting smarter, but sellers are also advertising places to unplug. Zillow’s 2027 Home Trends Report finds growing mentions of both AI and tech-free spaces, along with color-drenched rooms, home offices, and “catios.”

The report tracks year-over-year changes in mentions of hundreds of features across millions of for-sale listings. The results reflect how sellers and agents are marketing homes—not a survey of buyers or evidence that a particular feature will help a property sell. Still, they offer a look at the amenities and design details increasingly making their way into listing descriptions. Here are the main takeaways.

Mentions of AI are up 119 percent, the largest increase in this year’s report. Zillow points to AI-powered appliances among the features sellers are advertising, while mentions of app-controlled home systems rose 34 percent.

Spaces to unplug are also appearing 34 percent more often. The report describes an interest in both technology that simplifies daily tasks and designated places to take a break from it.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert, in a statement. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Sellers and their real estate agents are now highlighting spaces to unplug.”

Color-drenched spaces—rooms where the same color extends across the walls, ceiling, and trim—are up 84 percent in listing mentions, making them one of the report’s biggest increases. Zillow sees the approach as part of a move toward bolder, more polished interiors. Exterior finishes are gaining ground, too: painted brick is up 27 percent, and board-and-batten siding rose 24 percent.

Color drenching takes a shade beyond the walls and onto the ceiling, trim, and other architectural details. The approach has even spawned a bathroom variation: tile drenching, which extends the same tile across surfaces for a continuous look.

The language used to describe these homes is changing alongside the features. Mentions of “elevated” rose 71 percent, while “cozy” fell 22 percent, “eclectic” dropped 19 percent, and “boho” declined 13 percent. Cozy remains one of the most common words in listings, but it’s appearing less frequently than it did a year ago.

Kitchen language is moving in the other direction. Mentions of “chef’s kitchens” fell 21 percent, and “gourmet kitchens” declined 15 percent.

Mentions of multigenerational living jumped 58 percent. Zillow connects the increase to homes accommodating the “sandwich generation,” or people caring for children and older family members.

Features that can serve different ages and needs are also appearing more frequently. Grab bars are up 18 percent, wider doorways are up 13 percent, and nurseries rose 9 percent. The report places these changes alongside the growing emphasis on flexible rooms and spaces with more than one use.

The home spa is changing. Mentions of spa-inspired bathrooms rose 60 percent, and cold plunge pools increased 44 percent. Jacuzzis and jetted tubs, meanwhile, each fell about 9 percent.

Spaces for exercise are also gaining ground. Home gyms are up 18 percent, while golf simulators rose 29 percent. Together, the findings cover both workout spaces and amenities marketed around rest and recovery, rather than a single version of the home wellness room.

Cats get a category of their own this year. “Catios”—screened-in outdoor spaces that let indoor cats spend time outside—are appearing in 32 percent more listings. A place to get some air, watch the birds, and keep an eye on the neighbors—the cat has a wish list, too.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6954. Tom Hardy's 5 Most Stylish Characters, Ranked

From Inception's sharply-dressed con-man to the getaway driver in RocknRolla, here’s what you can learn from his on-screen wardrobe.

Tom Hardy doesn’t usually play the good guy. He’s been a forger, a fixer, a getaway driver, a rogue spy, and, in one of his more ambitious films, both halves of the most notorious twins in British crime (not that there’s much competition). Most of them are walking red flags, sure, but they’re red flags in great clothes.

That’s partly down to the costume designers, who’ve given Hardy everything from ’60s East End tailoring to Steve McQueen-inspired ’70s outerwear, but it’s also down to Hardy, who has a particular knack for making a wardrobe feel like his own. Put him in a Western shirt or three-piece and he’ll look equally at home in both (and you’ll want to steal the look, either way).

So, we’ve dug through the archives to rank his best-dressed roles of all time, and one of them’s actually on TV right now. Consider it your new watch list—just ignore the red flags.

Everyone remembers the spinning top and Joseph Gordon-Levitt’s three-piece, but the best-dressed man in Inception is the one who usually gets overlooked: Tom Hardy’s Eames. Costume designer Jeffrey Kurland imagined him as an expat from a Graham Greene novel, and every piece was made from scratch (even though half of it looks straight off a Dries Van Noten runway). Think batik-print shirts, collars spread over relaxed blazers, sharp trousers, and a wallet chain—basically clothes for a guy who spends too long sitting in the sun and definitely doesn’t wear SPF. Then there’s the watch: a gold tank-style antique Kurland picked up from the prop master’s collection—and he’s since forgotten the brand. Reddit’s watch sleuths are still on the case, 16 years and counting.

Legend gave its costume department a very important task: how do you tell Tom Hardy apart from Tom Hardy? Lead designer Caroline Harris and Soho tailor Chris Kerr solved it with buttons: Reggie, the charmer, wears single-breasted suits that feel safe and easy to trust. Ronnie, meanwhile, is the volatile one who gets three-piece double-breasted silhouettes with extra padding to make him look macho. On both sides, though, everything’s cut from vintage fabrics for a lived-in look straight from the hanger. Sixty years on, you could walk into any hotel bar dressed as either and nobody would bat an eye. Until you start talking business, that is.

Inspired by the real-world Cold War scandals that rocked British Intelligence, Tinker Tailor Soldier Spy follows the hunt for a Russian mole inside MI6 in 1973. So, as you might expect, it’s lots of men doing stuff in gray suits. Not Hardy’s Ricki Tarr, though. He’s the only agent with any personality, wardrobe-wise, all thanks to costume designer Jacqueline Durran. Her thinking? Steve McQueen on a mission: a shearling coat in Istanbul, a Baracuta Harrington in Paris, and not a standard suit in sight. The kind of menswear every guy on your FYP is wearing today—Hardy was just 15 years early.

In MobLand, Pierce Brosnan and Helen Mirren head up the Harrigans, an Irish crime family at war on several fronts—and no one knows it better than Hardy. He plays Harry Da Souza, the guy they call when things go wrong, which seems to happen fairly often. Costume designer Rebecca Hale gave him a wardrobe for the job: a battered wax jacket when he’s taking care of the Cotswolds estate, say, or a big overcoat when he’s doing business in the city. There’s one exception to the low-key menswear staples, though: his watch. In season one, he wore a solid gold Rolex GMT-Master II, which is reportedly the most valuable watch in the show. Pretty good for the hired help.

MobLand isn’t Hardy’s first Guy Ritchie gangster part. In RocknRolla, he’s Handsome Bob, the Wild Bunch’s getaway driver. And while everyone else looks like they’d had someone ‘”taken care of” before lunch, Hardy’s low-key menswear staples make him look more like the boy next door. A faded denim shirt. A classic bomber with jeans. A blue checked Western shirt, open just far enough. All simple pieces that let his charm do its thing, which it does when he sweet-talks a lawyer into revealing who’s been grassing to the police. Four years earlier, he played Clarkie in Layer Cake, another member of a London crime crew but one with a very different wardrobe: slicked hair, blazers, open shirts, brandy, and cigars. Similar JD, just with a very different wardrobe.

In which we will be transported back to those glorious days for fourth-grade history class.

So who was this indiscreet fellow anyway? In Washington, Sparky, the mic is always hot, the camera is always on, and the person next to you on the subway has ProPublica’s phone number.

#6947. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6939. This Forgotten ’90s Comedy Is the Most Demented Thanksgiving Movie Ever

Ed O’Neill and Ethan Embry go on the road in Dutch but end up in some bizarre and disturbing places.

Let’s start off by acknowledging that the late John Hughes wrote and directed the greatest of all Thanksgiving movies with Planes, Trains and Automobiles. There are definitely other great ones, but … Dutch is a different kind of creature. This is a Thanksgiving family comedy in which a grown man fights, kidnaps, and abandons a child. Occasionally he holds him at gunpoint. It’s a rotten child but a kid nonetheless. The movie still made me laugh, but it was mostly in disbelief.

This road-trip saga that Hughes penned and produced in 1991 is by the numbers in most ways but excels in its brazen wrongness, even by the free-range standards of Generation X. I come here to praise this messy movie, not to bury it. It’s a relic from when films could be wilder, shaggier, and more shocking than the rigidly tame family comedies of today. Dutch is many things, but it is not refined to baby-food levels of inoffensive smoothness.

Hughes didn’t direct this one, handing off those duties to Peter Faiman, who had worked extensively in Australian TV and only made one other feature film: 1986’s Crocodile Dundee. I can’t help but feel this was an experiment gone wrong, or maybe a first draft that needed another pass from Hughes. It’s built out of leftovers from better road-trip movies, and like any Frankenstein’s monster, this one runs straight into a wall and ultimately falls apart.

But if you want something different for your Thanksgiving watch, go find this one. Fair warning: It’s not streaming anywhere in the United States, so you’ll have to seek it out on a sketchy Russian bootleg site that makes it look like the footage was ripped from a worn-down VHS.

Dutch stars Ed O’Neill as the title character, a blue-collar guy sent to pick up his girlfriend’s blue-blooded son from a Georgia boarding school and deliver him to Chicago in time for the holiday feast. The not-so-surprising catch is that the kid (played by a 13-year-old Ethan Embry) mercilessly despises Dutch, and honestly the feeling is mutual.

Doyle is one of the most hateful kids in comedy history. Even the ginger demon of Problem Child was more cartoonishly mischievous than sociopathic. Embry, who was then credited as Ethan Randall, isn’t playing a troublemaking scamp like the kid in O. Henry’s “The Ransom of Red Chief” or Hughes’s own Home Alone. He’s a smug sociopath with a violent mean streak.

We learn that Dutch is a salt-of-the-earth cousin to O’Neill’s uncouth Al Bundy from Married with Children because he flicks the caviar off his cracker at a fancy party. (“I make it a rule never to eat anything a fish deposits in a riverbed.”) Okay, fair.

Compare that to how we meet Doyle: Another young boy in his boarding school comes to his room to say he has a call on the dormitory’s community phone line. “Someone from any sort of decent background would knock,” Embry’s character hisses. He then corrects the kid’s vocabulary and threatens to report him to the school administrators for mild cursing. So he’s a snob, a prick, and a rat. But even this is within the bounds of film comedy. You could picture an older version of him as a stuck-up villain in Caddyshack or Animal House.

Then there’s another, even more disturbing phone scene when Doyle gets the bad news that his rich father won’t be helping him get out of this road trip with Dutch. He hangs up and stalks past an elderly janitor who is sweeping the hallway, silently kicking over the garbage can to scatter trash across the helpless old man’s feet. There’s no joke. He’s just a son of a bitch.

Doyle would fit in great with the Children of the Corn, except he’d scowl at their shabby farm clothes.

Obviously this kid is going to get some form of comeuppance. When Dutch shows up at his dorm room to start the trip, Doyle beats him with a golf club and shoots him in the testicles with a BB gun. Something about this is much more violent and visceral than the Looney Tunes–style pratfalls and clobberings of Home Alone.

They have a fistfight in the tiny room, and Dutch seizes the weapon. Does he toss it away or break it? No—he uses it himself to hold the kid at gunpoint. We have just crossed beyond the boundaries of comedy, never to return. Dutch then ties the boy up, lashes him to a hockey stick for easier carrying, and stuffs underwear in his mouth to muffle his cries.

You know a movie is going off the rails when watching it makes you feel like you should be testifying against the characters as a witness for the prosecution.

So begins the drive, with Dutch and Doyle doing their best to annoy each other verbally. Then comes a bizarre scene in which Dutch stops for fireworks for some reason and sets them off in a snowy field while the kid watches from the car. I suppose he’s trying to inspire a little playfulness in the boy, but it just seems like Dutch has lost it and is indulging a pyromaniac streak.

It goes wrong and he sets his coat on fire, which makes it harder to understand why he loses his temper when the boy flicks the butt of his lit cigar at him. The man just went mano a mano with a whizzing fireworks spinner, but this is what lights his fuse?

Dutch does the reasonable thing. He abandons the boy in the snowy night and tells him to walk the rest of the way to the motel. “About 50 miles down the road!” Fifty miles? Okay, Dutch, I believe we’ve added another felony to the charges.

This isn’t one of those moments where the grown-up pulls over just beyond the tree line and waits for him. Dutch actually goes to the hotel, although he occasionally puts down his paperback and checks the window to see if the boy has made it there alive. When Doyle finally does show up, shivering and wet, Dutch says, “Here’s something you should know about me. I don’t fool around. You piss me off, I react.”

Uncle Buck certainly had its off-color and irresponsible moments, but even that havoc-prone babysitter might have called Child Protective Services over this situation.

Doyle responds by stealing Dutch’s car keys while he sleeps and parking the sedan sideways in the snowy road, where it is slammed into by an 18-wheeler. This is a comedy, so the hapless truck driver is not killed on impact.

Faiman shoots all of this with too much gritty realism. Maybe it would work if it was all a little zanier and over the top (think of Vacation), but Dutch just plays less like a series of set pieces and more like an array of crime scenes. They don’t call anyone for help because Dutch doesn’t want to admit to his girlfriend how badly things are going, so they hitchhike the rest of the way and stay in a homeless shelter, where the rotten kid learns that a lot of people have it much worse than he does.

At one point, they are picked up by a pair of pretty young women who are driving home from a job at the Superdome in New Orleans. “We’re call girls,” one of them explains. Hoo boy. Here we go.

But thankfully—mercifully—Dutch goes no further than showing the kid sleeping in the backseat with his head resting on the chest of one of the women. Not great, but it could be worse. (Worse is when Dutch tries to bond with the child over a deck of naked playing cards.)

Even Dutch seems to recognize that “prostitute road trip” probably isn’t the ideal situation for a young boy. But he explains it in the most vulgar way possible: “You and I are riding in the backseat from now on, because what that missile twister can teach you, you don’t need to learn until you’re in prison.” Then the ladies drive off without them, taking Dutch’s wallet and leaving the pair stranded again.

They spend the next leg of their trip riding in the open concrete pipes in the back of a long-haul truck, but they fall asleep and wake up in a trailer depot where they are roughed up by two security guards. Doyle responds by drawing his BB gun on them and making them believe he is going to blow their heads off.

This same joke was deployed by Chevy Chase in the Hughes-scripted National Lampoon’s Vacation (“Are you kidding, this is a Magnum P.I.”) but here, again, it just plays as psychotic violence rather than absurd humor. “The kid is crazy,” Dutch tells the men as they beg for their lives.

By the time the duo finally get home for the Thanksgiving meal, the hostility between Dutch and the kid has softened into a grudging respect. I know: Surprise! Back at the mother’s house, Dutch physically assaults the boy’s callous father, leaving a mark with his pinkie ring on the jerk’s forehead. Instead of an assault charge, it all ends happily ever after with a callback to an earlier gag: Dutch pulls the BB pistol from his pocket and says it’s time to get the revenge he promised he would one day seek. As the boy’s smile fades, Dutch aims the gun at him—and us (!!!)—as the film cuts to the credits.

Oh, Dutch … you dangerously inappropriate maniac. Don’t ever change. And, well, he doesn’t, although the kid goes through the expected metamorphosis to become a more sensitive and understanding human.

They don’t make ’em like this anymore, as the saying goes, and I actually think that’s a pity. Dutch isn’t all that funny, but neither is toothless humor that’s overly safe. I’d gladly tolerate a few misfires like this if it meant more truly unhinged comedies like we had in the ’80s, ’90s, and early 2000s. Dutch stands as a throwback to when small-scale comedies weren’t afraid to take big swings—even if they occasionally landed against someone’s skull or testicles.

#6935. Blade Runner 2099 Is a Beautiful Slog Through a Grim Future

The new Prime Video series picks up way, way after Denis Villeneuve’s sequel. Not that it matters.

Crowded streets. Long, cool coats. Flying cars darting through smog and neon skyscrapers. An abundance of Japanese aesthetics that imply America’s lost superpower status. Ridley Scott’s seminal 1982 masterwork Blade Runner (itself adapted from Philip K. Dick’s Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep?) laid the foundation for almost all of cyberpunk science fiction, so much so that everything else feels undeservedly derivative. Even a new Blade Runner. The new series Blade Runner 2099 looks the part, but it lacks the soul of its predecessors, leaving its perspectives on what it means to be more human than human hollow, like a Replicant without memories of its own.

Streaming now on Prime Video (and just in time for a long holiday weekend) is Blade Runner 2099. A stand-alone continuation of both Ridley Scott’s towering original and Denis Villeneuve’s remarkable legacy sequel, the series takes place in 2099 (decades after Villeneuve’s Blade Runner 2049). An unseen revolution has elevated Replicants to the ruling class of Los Angeles, rendering flesh-and-bone meat-bag humans as the new discriminated minority.

Hunter Schafer flexes some of her budding leading-lady chops as Cora, a real human posing as a Replicant to smuggle herself and her brother out of Los Angeles. Her ruse leads her to Olwen (Oscar-winner Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant Blade Runner spending the final days of her artificial life span seeking out someone from her past. A humorless buddy-cop mystery, Blade Runner 2099 is mired in a plot-centric narrative that cares less about the why of all things—an underrated and beautiful feature of its predecessors—than the what and how.

In other words, it’s the first time a piece of Blade Runner media actually zeroes in on the plot, instead of anything else more interesting. Try not to think of this as an eight-hour Blade Runner series but rather an eight-hour series stretching at the seams to feel worthy of its branded title.

Is it because streaming TV in 2026 has become so plastic and hollow that even Blade Runner 2099 feels like an unengaging slog? Or were the direction of both Scott and Villeneuve so immaculate that we were distracted from how turgid they might have been all this time? Regardless, 2099’s lack of auteurship is its most deafening shortcoming. The series has been steered to the screen by executive producer Silka Luisa, whose warmer color palettes—based on her choice of more sunshine, which glistens off surfaces—lend 2099 some identity. Some.

But beyond even that minor distinction, Blade Runner 2099 is still just a plot-y crime drama that checks off boxes and goes through the motions of its genre, with nary a real thing to say. About us, about our fragile humanity, about the demise of all we’ve built through artificial intelligence. Blade Runner 2099 is a dystopian sci-fi in all the ways you expect it, but it’s hardly a good one. Sure, the original movies were oppressive, dreary, and morbid too. But they possessed a knowing glare into our fears about the future and our imminent collapse as a civilized species. The new series lacks this same insight, or indeed any of its own, all in the name of goosing up engagement numbers for the closest thing to a real-life Tyrell Corp. (Do shareholders dream of electric quarterly reports?)

Despite surface-level similarities to the movies, plus a moody noir-like motif with a sexy saxophone that, admittedly, completely and unapologetically rips, the plodding Blade Runner 2099 is a needless walkabout through a world that never begged for one. Needless is a good word, actually. The show’s strong visual execution is negated by its inability to rise to the occasion as a worthwhile or enlightening probe of the human experience, or even just as an entertaining cop show. It may be smart, but what good is intelligence if there isn’t an ounce of grace?

#6930. You Don’t Need Designer Sneakers. You Need These $95 German Army Trainers.

We must admit that these ones from Quince are damn good.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

In my life, I lean toward luxury and secondhand clothing wherever possible, desperately trying to get away from all the cheap garbage being sold to me on Instagram ads and the Amazon homepage. I’ve spent years of my life hunting sales and eBay listings for clothes and boots. I’m like an edge-habitat pine growing in the direction of the radiant sun that is well-made clothing, trying to forget the fast fashion slop that lies in the forest behind me.

But I throw that out the window when it comes to sneakers. Designer sneakers are, for me, one of the dumbest things I could ever buy. Even when they are made with real leather, you are paying for a plastic sole that has a lifespan of a few years. I don’t think you should pay more than the $200 price of a pair of New Balance when it comes to sneakers.

Under that price point, I’ve never been able to find a good pair German Army Trainers, which I have always wanted. That changed when I found these. Quince (yeah yeah, I was just complaining about fashion slop, but life is full of contradictions) makes a perfect pair of GATs—and it makes them for $95. I honestly think every man in the world should buy a pair of these.

This is my real qualm with designer sneakers, and it totally undercuts anything you could tell me about the second-hand sneaker hype market. What you should pay for, when you pay expensive prices for clothing, is craftsmanship. When you buy a Gucci 1953 loafer or Loro Piana cashmere you probably arrived there because of the brand, but you are paying for the craftsmanship. I see no such correlation in designer sneakers, even the ones those brands make.

But a $95 pair of kicks? That’s honest. Even though I’m not bullish on Quince’s fast fashion take on quiet luxury (nor am I bullish on its caviar program), and the fact that Quince chose to closely replicate the Margiela GAT instead of the original German army models, I will let it slide. At $95, there are no claims being made about leather quality, or the footbed, or whatever is going on in the sole you can’t see. They look good and are easy to replace; it’s about as honest of a deal as you can find shopping these days.

The thing about the original BW Sport GATs—which you can get for about this price still, though used workout sneakers aren’t my cup of tea—is that they’re just about bulletproof. Those original mid-century designs made were first issued in the 1970s and stopped being issued around the end of the Cold War, a real feat of durability.

These shoes mimic that Army-issued durability. I’ve worn these sneakers for going on a year now. In that time, I’ve seen many pairs of more expensive kicks start to fall apart, get scuffed beyond recognition, and stink like hell. These have not. The pair I’ve worn all year is the pair pictured in this story. Nothing I say in this endorsement could be more convincing than that.

And really that’s the argument here. When I wrote about the Chuck Taylors I normally wear, it was a use-it-and-lose-it argument—they cost $45, who cares if the soles get holes within 12 months. But here, I fear I’m going to sound like a marketing mouthpiece for Quince. These shoes are $95, but they’ll last just as long as a pair that cost you $950. That’s all you can ask for these days.

#6927. Amazon Has Some Incredible Deals on Levi’s Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Up to 55% off? Black Friday isn’t messing around this year.

It's easy for me, the style writer, to say you shouldn't determine whether or not you buy something based on how much it is (or is not) on sale. I know how the real world works, though. I write about denim all the time, and I use every opportunity I get to recommend the fancy stuff. Honestly, though, I spend most days in a pair of $40 Wranglers that I bought on sale for $25, because I also am a regular American trying to get by. Used to be, good denim only cost a few bucks. Now, it's lower quality and costs more money. Reproductions of what used to be American working clothes now easily cost $300 or more. C'est la vie.

But I'm not here to talk about all that. I'm here to say like with my Wranglers, it's OK to wear cheap jeans. You can still be stylish if your pants cost $50. This month’s Black Friday Amazon deals on Levi’s prove it.

We've got the 505, the modern Levi’s fit I think works for most men, that's selling for between $45 and $55—depending on the wash you want and size you need. The 517, Levi's take on a Western cut, is going for a discount. And the 501—which I don't love in its modern iteration, but cannot deny the popularity of—is hovering just below $50, depending on the size and wash. Depending on the jeans you buy, you’re getting a discount of between 19 and 43 percent for the best cheap denim out there. If you're looking for a new jacket, which Levi's still makes decent versions of, Amazon has deals on two sherpa trucker jackets. Go with the classic denim for $63 or opt for the corduroy for $85.

All right, that's enough explaining. Pick what speaks to you. Buy it. Then, go forth a new man with refreshed style.

#6915. Omega and Swatch Just Launched a New MoonSwatch—and No Two Are the Same

The Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake, the 39th watch in the series, will have collectors keeping an eye on the Swiss weather forecast.

By MoonSwatch standards, it’s been a quiet year. Swatch has been otherwise occupied with the Audemars Piguet Royal Pop, the May release for which people lined up for six days in New York, police deployed tear gas in France, and which, according to Swatch Group, has generated more than 25 billion views on social media.

Not that interest in the MoonSwatch has gone away.

An estimated 4.5 million have been sold since the collaboration began in March 2022 (and that figure only takes us to the end of 2024). In July this year, some 1.2 million people applied to buy one of the 1,969 examples of its $570 Mission to the Moon 1969, a watch that incorporated 11 grams of 18-carat gold recycled from vintage Omega components.

The latest MoonSwatch—only the third new model this year, compared with 11 in 2023—is the Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake.

An all-black counterpart to last winter’s all-white Cold Moon edition, it takes as its starting point the idea that no two snowflakes are alike.

Swatch has developed a digital process capable of generating millions of different snowflake patterns, one of which is laser-engraved onto the moonphase indicator of each watch. So, unusually for a mass-produced quartz watch, every example is unique.

It also features the complication Swatch introduced in November 2024 and which remains exclusive to the MoonSwatch: an earthphase indicator. Where a conventional moonphase shows the phases of the Moon as viewed from Earth, this does the reverse. The two displays run in opposite directions over the same 29.5-day cycle.

(How useful this is to those of us who live on Earth is debatable, but it’s one of the more original ideas in watchmaking in recent years.)

There are some nice details elsewhere on the 42mm Bioceramic watch, including Snoopy and Woodstock in the snow, a pair of miniature skis at 11 o’clock, and a tiny patch of white on the Earth representing a snow-covered Switzerland, which glows green under UV light.

As with last year’s winter edition, there’s a stipulation.

The watch goes on sale at selected Swatch stores worldwide on Black Friday, November 27. After that, until March 20, 2027, the last day of winter, you’ll only be able to buy one when it’s snowing in Switzerland.

Swatch will be providing weather updates on its website.
