#6911. The 6 Best Gaming Headsets for All Types of Players

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re playing single-player or online, these will level up your game.

Nothing truly makes me feel immersed in a game the same way good sound does. And it’s impossible to get that level of tension in a horror game like Resident Evil, or the feeling of being absolutely locked in a competitive shooter without a good pair of headphones. Nowadays, video games are fully open world of 3D and spatial audio, and there's never been a better time to hop on the bandwagon. It's time to get a gaming headset.

If you consider gaming your main hobby, you’re probably either a single-player person or you like to squad up in your battle royale of choice. It doesn’t matter, actually, because the only proper way to experience both ends of the gaming spectrum is with a headset. For single-player, that means fully immersive audio. For multiplayer, you’ll sound crystal clear to your teammates—no more confusion or excuses. The good noise-canceling headsets even have transparency modes so you don’t have to ignore everyone in your real life either.

Here's the problem. There are actually plenty of good headsets and trustworthy brands that make dozens of similar options. Finding the headset that will work for you isn’t made to be easy, but that’s why I’m here. I know my gaming headsets like the back of my hand—that’s my job. To figure out if you need a high-end wireless SteelSeries headset, or if you’ll be happy with a sub-$100 option, read on. Hell, maybe you’d prefer earbuds. I’ve accounted for all options.

This is the one gaming headset I won’t (and frankly can’t) say anything negative about. I suppose that’s what $600 gets you. SteelSeries has long made comfortable headsets that support multiple platforms with easy swapping. It introduced the world to its GameHub base station, letting you connect all your systems to one panel and easily control them all from there.

The Nova Elite takes every already perfectly solved puzzle piece and puts a little extra sheen on it. First off, it is gorgeous, with exteriors made from premium steel, aluminum, and vegan leather. Nothing else in the gaming space compares to it in terms of the luxe look. In terms of fit, the Nova Elite distributes its weight evenly so you can play for hours without discomfort.

And this headset’s gaming hub has an extra feature that really changes things. The hot-swappable batteries are SteelSeries’ biggest innovation in recent years, and here the hub also acts as a charger for your second (included) headset battery. So whichever one is not powering your current session is being charged. What this means is battery life is a solved problem. You literally never have to worry about running out of charge mid-game again. And for some, that alone might be worth the price of admission.

Okay, forget what I just said. This is everything a wireless headset needs to be for the average gamer. ASUS has targeted a lower price point and succeeded in terms of knowing which features to cut, and what to keep to make it still feel like a high-quality experience. You don't get active noise cancellation or the ability to play audio from two sources at once. For those luxuries, go for the Delta II.

At 0.7 pounds, the Pelta is remarkably light and the furthest thing from bulky. It's about letting you play all day on those summer Saturdays where it's too hot to go outside. Where it surprises in this regard is battery life. Offering up to 70 hours between charges and a fit that won't leave you itchy and sweaty, this is the all-nighter-friendly headset.

While I wouldn't personally spring for a wired headset, those who do are hard to convince otherwise. And that crowd has spent the last couple of years rallying around the HyperX Cloud Alpha. If competitive gaming is the reason you are turning to a headset, this has everything you need for under $100.

You aren't going to get the audio options you do from the multi-hundred-dollar headsets SteelSeries and Corsair sell. Instead, you are paying a reasonable price for a high-quality microphone, and a comfortable pair of headphones. Stop worrying about lag and stop worrying about your headset running out of battery. It's time to wire in.

When it comes to sound quality, the Corsair Virtuoso Max brings it to a new level. This is a better-sounding headset than most of the readers of this article have ever experienced.

My favorite high-tech touch that we actually use is the simultaneous connection. Using the 2.4 GHz dongle and Bluetooth in tandem you can get the audio from your game and audio from a Bluetooth source at the same time. That means pumping in your own tunes or a background podcast while grinding levels. When playing with friends, I love the addition of a physical, sturdy, functional mic mute button.

The only issue is long-term comfort. Weighing in at just under a pound, the 60-hour battery life on the headset is a bit of a moot point. You'll want to take it off anyway because it can start to feel quite hard on your skull after a couple hours of white knuckle gaming.

If you have a single gaming machine and that console is a PS5, this is the headset I recommend. It is remarkably lightweight, weighing even less than the wired-only pair I recommended earlier, and it’s super easy to use—with a satisfying mute button that pops in and out, and a microphone that is entirely detachable. So if you’re settling in for a night of solo horror gaming, you can set the mic aside and still enjoy the excellent sound and spatial audio options. And if you know that battery is low, or you really just want the easiest solution, the package includes a 3.5mm cable for wired connection. Just plug that right into your DualSense controller and you’re off to the races.

Beyond just PlayStation diehards, this is an excellent option for any PC or Nintendo gamer who wants a light wireless headset. It is fully compatible via USB-C transceiver or headphone cable with any other modern console, sans the Xbox.

Okay, so I lied. When you clicked on this for the six best headsets, yeah one of these isn't a gaming headset at all. It's a set of earbuds designed by SteelSeries to be used on gaming consoles and it's as much of a game-changer as it sounds. If you feel cheated, don't fret. There are two more headsets listed in the honorable mentions below. Please accept my humble offering.

If you’ll allow me to talk about them for just a minute though, the Arctis GameBuds are just shy of a miracle. They are comfy earbuds with all-encompassing noise-canceling (gasp!), spatial audio support, and a very useful transparency/talk-through mode. The sound isn't quite on par with SteelSeries's larger headsets, but it still impresses. Plus, unlike the rest of these headsets, you can fit them in your pocket.

SteelSeries Arctis Nova 7 ($179): You don't need to shell out for the Nova Elite to get a good Arctis headset. This one uses a simple USB-C dongle, so isn't nearly as complicated to set up, either.

Turtle Beach Stealth Pro 2: ($349): My favorite new headset of the year. An excellent multi-platform choice with high-quality playback and call audio. It also uses a swappable battery system, so you never run out of charge in a heated gaming moment.

Testing gaming headsets really isn't too difficult. You make sure it's compatible with what it says on the box, and then tell the people if it’s comfortable and if it sounds good. Shit simple until you do it enough to realize a simple truth. Most models from reputable brands are worth their costs, and very few companies have duds in their lineups. That said, there are ways to tell what's going to rise from the crop. Here's some pointers for the next time you shop for headsets.

Comfort — If you can't stay comfortable in them for more than an hour they are going straight in the trash. Even the most uncomfortable among these picks are still in the top 5% of comfortable gaming headphones.

Sound Quality — Like with the pair of headphones you use to listen to music, you want clear sound with plenty of range.

Microphone — If you're gaming online, your microphone needs to cut out unwanted background noise. Some offer noise cancelling, some just have good unidirectional builds. All on this list are great in this department.

Directional Sound — Spatial audio is cool when watching movies, but when playing games this can take your team play to the next level. When you hear an enemy's footsteps, you can know they're behind you at four o'clock, not six. And that makes you an asset. It also makes the taste of victory that much sweeter.

Connection — I will discuss wireless vs. wired shortly, but when it comes to the going wireless there's usually two ways to connect a gaming headset. Bluetooth or via a USB dongle. The latter typically has less latency and is the preferred way to connect.

Are wired or wireless headsets better for gaming?

If you care about latency (aka lag), we are going to recommend wired over wireless. The added hassle of wireless charging and connecting doesn't necessarily make up for the hassle of having a wire. Plus, you don't have to worry about it not working on some devices—which you do with pretty much every wireless device you buy.

Do all gaming headsets work across all platforms?

I'm glad you asked. No! As you may have noticed, there tend to be two kinds of headsets. Those that work with Xbox.. and those that work with everything else. Since Xbox uses its own wireless standard instead of Bluetooth. In practice, this means Xbox gamers need to be extra careful when buying headsets to make sure the one they're getting is compatible. SteelSeries, for example, sells two versions of each headset, one marked for Xbox.

It's actually more complicated than "all headsets that work with PC also work with PlayStation and you have to get ones especially for Xbox," but in many of these cases that is the gist. Although the PlayStation headsets work on PC and Mac but not Nintendo Switch. So, yeah, it's pretty inconsistent. Seriously, just read the list of compatible platforms before you buy a headset.

#6897. 13 Pairs of Gloves to Keep Your Digits Toasty All Winter Long

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Don’t overlook your cold-weather style practicalities.

There are few winter experiences more miserable than stepping outside with bare hands and realizing, too late, that your fingers have stopped working. First they’re freezing, then they’re numb, and before long your skin feels dry and rough and beyond the help of any hand lotion.

But honestly? Gloves can be annoying, too. Some turn a quick phone check into a whole production; others are so bulky you might as well be wearing oven mitts. The best winter gloves keep your hands warm without making your fingers useless—and look good while they’re at it. We’ve rounded up our favorites, from waterproof, windproof pairs built for rough weather to stylish options that still keep your hands nice and toasty. And yes, plenty have touchscreen-compatible fingertips so you can still call and text (and play the Wordle). See our favorite pairs below.

These luxurious leather gloves come from Overland, the brand behind some of our favorite coats. The smooth leather looks very polished, so these gloves can be dressed up if you want, but they go just as well with a casual outfit. They’re also quite durable and are naturally water-repellant, and the cashmere lining adds warmth on the inside.

Commerce Editor Luke Guillory loves these merino wool-lined deerskin gloves from Geier Gloves, a brand that has been making leather products in the U.S. for almost a century. Since the brand specializes in leather, you can bet the deerskin on these gloves is of the highest quality, and the wool lining is very nice, too. Geier also makes a lot of wor, driving, and motorcycle gloves, so you know they’re the real deal. The black and brown colorways look quite polished, but the natural and saddle leather options are also great.

If you want a nice luxurious pair of cashmere gloves, go with these ones from Sunspel. Lately, I’ve been wearing these gloves, which were knitted in Scotland from super soft pure cashmere. The brand has been perfecting the craft for over 150 years, so you can trust that these are the best of the best. It’s basically like enveloping your hands in a cozy blanket. For a full set, get the brand’s cashmere hat and scarf.

These Canada Goose gloves are basically the equivalent of wearing a puffer jacket on your hands. With heavy-duty, long rib-knit cuffs for extra protection and 750-fill-power down on the inside, your hands will stay warm and dry without their mobility being super impacted by something extra bulky. Note that these have a touchscreen-compatible index finger but not thumb.

Wool-blend gloves are as handsome as any of the very best sweaters in your rotation. Even though the outside is made from knit material, the insulated interior will keep your hands warm and dry during your next epic snowball fight. They’re also thin enough to layer under another pair of gloves if it’s really freezing outside.

When it comes to gloves, there’s one brand I’ve trusted for years, and that’s Smartwool. Their gloves are warm without being bulky, and mine have lasted for years. Having a warm pair of gloves that actually does its job well is even more important for my boyfriend, who has Raynaud’s and needs extra protection for his hands, and the gloves he relies on year after year? Also Smartwool. These new fleece gloves from the brand have extra insulation on the back to provide serious warmth, and they’re also functional with touchscreen-compatible pads on the thumbs and index fingers. Sure, they’re a little expensive for fleece gloves at $68—but if you don’t need the extra insulation, you can try the regular Active Fleece Gloves for $48.

If all you need is a solid pair of knit gloves and you’re not looking to spend a fortune, look no further than Uniqlo. The brand’s Heattech knit gloves are soft and warm and have effective touchscreen-compatible fingertips. They also come in 11 fun colors, so you might as well get a couple pairs for yourself—and they make a great holiday gift for literally anyone.

You already know we love Barbour’s waxed canvas jackets, so it’s no surprise we’re also a fan of the brand’s stylish, preppy gloves. These leather-palmed gloves have a cotton tartan face for a cool contrast and wool lining on the inside for extra warmth—and they’re waterproof, so you can break them out no matter what’s going on with the weather. Pair these with the Barbour Beaufort jacket for an iconic look.

Knit gloves are great when it comes to keeping warm, but they’re not the best when it’s raining or snowing. No one wants a pair of soaking gloves (that probably make your hands feel even colder). Enter these waterproof gloves from the brand that brought you the famous Bean Boots. With a waterproof nylon exterior and a down fill, they’ll keep rain and show out while keeping your hands warm. Note that some say the fingers are long, so consider that when choosing a size.

When you’re stuck in a blizzard or your location is prone to unpredictable weather, you’ll need something a little more heavy duty. REI’s weatherproof gore-tex gloves will give your hands the protection they need. They’re also essentially a two-in-one: The removable fleece liners can be used on their own or with the weatherproof outers as an extra layer of warmth.

Naadam’s famous cashmere sweater is one of our favorite pieces of winter clothing and one of our go-to gifts for the holidays, birthdays, Valentine’s Day, etc. The brand makes a matching pair of soft cashmere gloves, which come in 13 colors and are under $100. A genius gift idea? Get them the sweater with a matching pair of gloves, and they’ll wear both all winter long.

If you want that classic Sherlock Holmes leather glove look, you have lots of choices. But if you don’t want to spend a ton on a pair, get these $60 gloves from Quince. Not only do they have a nice leather exterior in either brown or black, they also have a cashmere lining on the inside.

On the high end of the high end, Gucci has a gorgeous luxury pair of leather gloves with the brand’s signature horsebit on the wrist for a fun little detail. Made from leather and silk, these are soft, smooth, and very stylish.

#6892. Of Course Trump Found a Way to Plug His Big, Ugly Ballroom When Partaking in a Beloved Thanksgiving Tradition

The idea of turkeys in that Gilded Palace of Sin is just too damn on the nose.

(Alternative Musical Accompaniment to the Last Post of the Week from the Blog’s Favourite Living Canadian)

We’re battening down here for the predicted Thanksgiving Gale. (The famous Perfect Storm is known as the Halloween Gale. We name storms like that. We are fcking adorable that way.) Apparently, this mother is going to slam the entire northeast corridor from Washington to Maine. Which means massive chaos at a number of very busy airports. And given the performance of the secretary of transportation and the secretary of homeland security when a squirrel recently got into the wiring in El Dorado, Arkansas, I have never been more glad not to be traveling this weekend. At least we will be spared another goony-bird press conference from Sean Duffy and Markwayne Mullin, referred to by writer and musician David Ferguson immortally as Dumb and Plumber. That’s a keeper.

It’s going to be tough to fly, tougher to take a train, and even tougher than that to drive. And the storm is going to lock people indoors with their obnoxious relatives, which can only lead to alcoholic embarrassments and domestic mayhem. Of course, it always could be worse. We’re all being stuck in the house by the elements of the natural world, whereas the folks in Iowa and all over the Midwest are being gifted with a cornucopia of toxic clouds for the holiday. From The New York Times:

What’s worse? Being stuck in the house with Meemaw, who found the bourbon, or being stuck in the family van, heading south, with Meemaw, who found the bourbon? The Army’s got your back, Iowa, but pack a bag and scout out a route to Honduras anyway. Wow.

And I’m sure this last-minute FEMA warning didn’t confuse anyone.

Don’t just do something, dammit, stand there.

And not for nothing, but the Army’s been acting sketchy about this whole business almost from jump. From The Washington Post:

Did the president use the charming tradition of pardoning a turkey for Thanksgiving as an excuse for plugging his Gilded Palace of Sin? (Thanks, Gram.) What do you think? From Axios:

Turkeys in that ballroom are just too damn on the nose.

The 2026 midterm elections continue to bless us. Representative Scott DesJarlais, perhaps the worst human ever elected to the House of Representatives, is gone baby gone, beaten by Democratic candidate Victoria Broderick. DesJarlais’s rap sheet is nigh unprecedented. This all came out in 2012, and the voters kept sending this guy back to Washington by fat margins until two weeks ago. He lost to Broderick, a proudly pansexual 34-year-old mother of two whom he beat handily in 2024. However, since then, the Republican Tennessee legislature brilliantly redrew the maps, adding slices of Nashville to the Fourth Congressional District, a change that benefitted Broderick. So not only is Congress liberated from the likes of DesJarlais, but Broderick’s win was also another example of how Republican ratfcking of the redistricting process bit them on the ass. Heh, heh, heh.

Weekly WWOZ Pick to Click: “Happy Days” (McCoy Tyner): Yeah, I pretty much still love New Orleans.

Weekly Visit to the Pathé Archives: Here, from 1940, is FDR celebrating Thanksgiving with the patients at the Warm Springs polio facility where he once was a patient. The narrator makes a funny about the U.S. joining “the war” against polio. FDR chats up a young patient who is chowing down on a turkey leg that is as big as her head. It looks like it was fun to have a real president.

Discovery Corner: Hey, look what we found! From Live Science:

I have to say, a “floating castle” sounds like a terrible idea, and wearing chain mail on a floating castle sounds like an even worse one. If the floating castle blows up and sinks, then it’s fairly hard to swim in chain mail.

Old King Hans has come down as a jolly old soul, but he was quite the political schemer. He also died in one of those prosaic ways kings died in those days. He fell from his horse.

Hey, SciNews, is it a good day for dinosaur news? It’s always a good day for dinosaur news!

Once again, I point out that paleontology doesn’t necessarily require sweating over some limestone cliff face. Grab a sandwich and a soda and hit the drawers of the file cabinets in museum basements. Surprises like this make me happy they lived then.

In honor of the holiday, I will not close with the usual recitation of the nation’s woes. But do spare a thought for the people—and the cows, and the sheep—under the plume in the Midwest. Make sure, however, to be grateful for all those things that warm your heart and make all your lights shine. And all say amen.

#6886. How Does Owen Wilson Find His Zen?

“I just believe there’s something more going on that you can tap into,” the actor tells Esquire on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.”

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Owen Wilson is a spiritual guy—and long has been. That’s why it fits so naturally that he would star in a golf series for Apple TV, Stick, in which his character, Pryce Cahill, is often searching to find his perfect zen. And as Wilson jokes, he’s started calling himself “Johnny Flowstate” on the green as he talks to himself through shots.

“I just believe there’s something more going on that you can tap into,” he tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The fifty-five-year-old actor, best known for his roles in The Royal Tenenbaums, Wedding Crashers, and Midnight in Paris, even admits that he found himself reaching into the beyond for advice from his late father, TV executive Robert Wilson, in a moment of pure panic before hosting Saturday Night Live in 2021.

“He was already gone at that point, but the night before I was freaking out like, I don’t know if I can do this. I was so nervous, and I was thinking a lot about my dad,” Wilson says. The two were incredibly close, and he reveals that his father actually worked with him and his brothers, actors Luke Wilson and Andrew Wilson, on several of their films. One of which was Wes Anderson’s Rushmore, in which their father wrote Bill Murray’s now-iconic church address to the students: “Take dead aim on the rich boys."

So, as Wilson found himself full of doubt and fear the night before hosting SNL, he recalls looking up and “literally asking [his father] for help” before falling asleep. “I woke up, just incredibly calm, and I had this conviction of this joke my dad would say that needed to go in my monologue.”

What was the joke? “That he was the charter member of the 3970 club,” Wilson says, “which stood for ‘three sons, nine high schools, seven colleges, and zero degrees.’”

“That wasn’t something he was proud of,” Wilson continues. “That was his way to cope with disappointment, but you could almost say that that joke was worth us having all those struggles in school because that joke has lived longer than another diploma would have.” You can watch the episode above as well as listen on Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

Luckily, it all worked out for the Wilson brothers. Next up, Owen is set to appear in season 2 of Stick on Apple TV and the Meet the Parents legacy sequel, Focker-in-Law with Ben Stiller. He also takes a liking to Esquire’s suggestion of a Christmas movie with another one of his longtime collaborators, Wes Anderson, in the episode.

“It’s one of the biggest relationships of my life,” Wilson says of his friendship with the director. “Had I gotten into one of the colleges that I applied to, I would never have met Wes. And there’s something I always say to my boys—the idea that you can’t do it alone. If there’s something going wrong, I know I can always call and he’ll always be there for me.”

#6884. Individual expression needs leadership, not restrictions

What happens when an educator’s individual expression becomes a management problem? Modern organizations encourage employees to express their individuality and contribute their unique perspectives to the workplace. While freedom can increase creativity, it also presents a challenge for management.
